- **Native modules** (zgx-script's `modules` feature, the new `zgx-module` crate): an app's own Rust built apart from zgx as a library and loaded as the app starts (`ScriptHost::load_module`). A module is a `mod` marked `#[zgx_module::module]` whose `#[export]` functions take Rust types (`&str`, numbers, `Option`, lists, maps, structs deriving `Record`); scripts call them as members of the module's table, `ui.module("words").count(text)`, typed in the editor from the module's manifest by `zgx types`, or by name with `ui.call_sync` and `ui.action`, untyped. A wrong argument is an error raised in the script naming the function, the module and the argument. A module links `zgx-module` and nothing of zgx, so a change to a module rebuilds that module alone, and it reads the runtime's values where they are, with no copy: a call into a loaded module costs 14 instructions more than the same function linked in. `zgx_module.h` is the same interface for C. Only the app's Rust loads one: no script or plugin can. [The guide](docs/modules.md); [the design](docs/design/runtime.md).
- **An app's clipboard can be its own, and `zgx inspect` drives it** (zgx-desktop, zgx-script, zgx). Under `ZGX_CLIPBOARD=memory` a window's clipboard is held in memory: text, HTML, files, an image and the primary selection. Copying and pasting never touch the person's clipboard, for QA and off-screen sessions. `ZGX_OFFSCREEN` sessions hold theirs in memory unless `ZGX_CLIPBOARD` names another (`system`). `zgx inspect clipboard` reads it, and `zgx inspect clipboard set --text … | --html … | --image file.png` sets it. `zgx inspect key cmd+v` (`ctrl+v` off macOS) now pastes as a window's key does: an editor's `on_paste` hears it, which a key sent to the view alone never reached. ⌘C and ⌘X work the same way (`Ui::clipboard_shortcut`, hidden).
- **`GpuRenderer::debug_time_passes`** (macOS): a frame drawn with Metal directly is split at its backdrop blur's passes, each committed on its own, and `debug_take_pass_times` gives each part's GPU time: where a frosted panel's cost lies. Off by default.
- **A node can lock the pointer, as the DOM's Pointer Lock** (zgx, zgx-desktop, zgx-script): a game's look about, a 3D view's orbit. `InputDispatcher::lock_pointer(scene, node)` (`NodeHandle::lock_pointer`, `el:lock_pointer()` in Luau) holds the pointer for the node: hidden and kept in place (winit's `CursorGrabMode::Locked`; `Confined` on X11, which has no lock), every press, release and wheel to the node wherever it is, and the mouse's raw motion as the new `InputEvent::PointerMotion { dx, dy }` (Luau: `on_pointer_move`'s `dx`, `dy`, with `locked = true`). Only within a second of a press, a release or a key, so nothing takes the pointer unasked; Escape always lets it go (and goes nowhere else), as do the window losing focus, the node leaving or being disabled, another node locking it and `unlock_pointer`. (A `match` naming every `InputEvent` handles both new variants, or adds a `_` arm.) The node hears `InputEvent::PointerLockChange` (Luau: `on_pointer_lock(locked)`), and the pointer comes back where it locked. Raw device events are listened for only while a pointer is locked, so other windows still read none. `on_pointer_move` also tells every move its `dx`, `dy` from the last, as the DOM's `movementX`.
- **The software raster can draw plain text as the GPU renderer does** (zgx-desktop, zgx-gpu): given the font system and text cache its scene is measured with (`Raster::share_text`), it lays out plain text from that cache and draws the GPU's glyph images on the GPU's pixels, with the same coverage firming and underlines (`zgx_gpu::plain_glyph_places`, `glyph_image`, `glyph_coverage`). Its frames match the GPU's to within blending's rounding: no channel off by more than 4 of 255 (1 on Metal); before, 5.9% of a text list's pixels differed and lines wrapped where layout hadn't. Without it, the raster draws as before.
- **A window can draw on the software raster where the only GPU is a software one** (zgx-desktop, zgx-gpu): with `ZGX_RENDERER=raster`, an opaque 1x window draws with the raster and shows its frames through softbuffer, copying only what changed. With `ZGX_RENDERER=auto` it does so where the machine has no hardware adapter, found without opening a device (`zgx_gpu::software_only`: no render node on Linux, only software adapters in DXGI on Windows), or where the GPU renderer's adapter turns out to be a software one. An idle app's resident memory fell from 140 MB on lavapipe to 42 MB, and a frame costs a fraction of the CPU (0.2–0.5 ms where lavapipe and WARP took 1.3–9.6). A window whose scale leaves 1x moves to the GPU. The GPU stays the default.
- **A disabled control can keep its look** (zgx-script): a control dimmed while disabled can be told not to be, for as long as a state or function says, without being built again; it stays disabled to input and assistive technology (a sending button that keeps its ink).
- **`ui.render_canvas(draw, { width, height, scale, x, y, background })` gives a drawing as a PNG** (zgx-script, zgx-gpu): `draw(c, w, h)` runs with the `c` a canvas's `draw` gets, and the promise gives what that canvas shows, in base64. Its shapes are rasterized as a canvas's are. Its text is shaped in the system's fonts and the app's own, as its windows shape it (`Platform::fonts`, which zgx-desktop answers with `Application::font`'s and `replace_font`'s), and drawn over the shapes. No window has to show it, so a plugin can picture a design for an agent or an export while the app is hidden. In Rust: `zgx_gpu::canvas::picture` and `canvas::png`.
- **`ui.mesh` takes its own triangles** (zgx-script): `geometry = { positions, indices, normals, colors }` draws a script's mesh (a CAD model, a scan, generated terrain), with `face_normals` and `face_colors` (or `flat = true`) for a flat-shaded, per-triangle look, and clicks hit its triangles as they do a named shape's. Inputs are checked with messages that say what's wrong, and a mesh holds at most 4,194,304 vertices as drawn. `material.double_sided` draws back faces too, for an open shell or a cut-away. Until now a script could only show the named shapes or a glTF file from its folder.
- **`ui.load_font(src)`** (zgx, zgx-script, zgx-gpu, zgx-desktop): a script loads a TrueType or OpenType file (a path from its directory, a `File` the user picked, or its bytes in base64: `{ base64 = … }`) and names its family in `font`, where text in a downloaded font (Inter, Geist) used to fall back to another face, measuring and drawing wrong. It reads off the main thread, takes up to 64 MiB, and rejects what is not a font. Every window's text in that family lays out and draws again (`Platform::add_font`, `WindowFactory::add_font`, `GpuContext::add_font`). A plugin needs the `fonts` grant (`Capability::Fonts`), and loads only from its own directory or a file the user picked.
- **A host lists what it offers its scripts** (zgx-script, hidden for now): `ScriptHost::registry()` gives the names of its actions, functions, async actions and shared keys (those its scripts made included), each list sorted, for an app to keep a record of what its scripts can reach.
- **A script shows a view the host makes in Rust** (zgx-script): `zgx_script::host_view(name, || View)` registers a view and `ui.host_view { name = "…" }` mounts it in the script's tree, so it lays out and clips like the script's own elements and stays under its popups, dialogs and layers, as a view composed beside the script's can't. A name nothing registered is an empty box.
- **A host can swap its scripts in place, and try them first** (zgx-script, hidden for now): `ScriptHost::from_files` runs scripts held in memory (`MemoryFiles`), as a bundle's, and freed with the last host holding them; `retarget` loads another set in place of the app's, its interface built again with its state kept, and if that load fails nothing changes; `validate` loads a set in a host and runtime of its own, touching nothing of the app's. Both say whether a load failed or ran past its time budget (`ReloadError`). A retarget reads the app's string catalog (`ui.i18n`) again from the new files; `MemoryFiles::new_at` keeps the root the app's first files had.
- **A scroll view can come to rest on whole pages** (zgx, zgx-script): `paging = true` on `ui.scroll_x` or `ui.scroll`, `View::paging` in Rust. On iOS it is `UIScrollView.pagingEnabled`, UIKit's own. On desktop a touch, or a touchpad where the platform says when fingers lift (macOS, Wayland), uses UIKit's spring and the speed that turns a page, a lift going at most one page from where it began; a mouse wheel, and a touchpad on X11 or Windows, goes a page a notch. Views that don't page scroll as they always have.
- **Presentation logs include scrolling's input and application** (zgx-desktop): `ZGX_PRESENT_LOG=1` stamps receipt, applied deltas, sampling margin and refresh period on the same clock as renderer and compositor feedback. Logging stays off by default.
- **A scroll view's or a list's scrollbar can be the app's own** (zgx, zgx-script): `scrollbar = { … }` in Luau and `View::scrollbar_style` in Rust set its thickness (under the pointer too), strip and side, how far its track stops short of each end, its shortest thumb, how long it lingers and fades (fading in too, on `outQuad`), a track shorter than the view, a hint that shows it, the theme's active thumb colour under the pointer, no track, a press beside the thumb that brings the thumb there, whether a press focuses it, and whether assistive technology has it, over the platform's look or zgx's own on every platform. An app's floating rail runs no script as its view scrolls: `track` and `hint` are read as what they read changes.
- **A selectable region says what a copy out of it puts on the clipboard** (zgx-script): `on_copy = function(request)` on a `selectable` node hears the selection's `text`, `html` and `leaves` (for each text node it spans, in order, the id of the element it is in inside the region, or `false`) and returns `{ text, html }`, or nil for the selection's own. ⌘C, the Edit menu and the selection's context menu all ask it; a region without one copies as before. For citations: a quote copied with its source.
- **A region's `on_copy` hears the list rows a selection spans** (zgx-script): `request.rows` holds, for each `ui.list` in the region the selection covers, `{ list, first, last }`: the list's id and its first and last rows, from 1, whether mounted or scrolled away. A Select All over a list of thousands names every row, so an app finds the citations of rows it never built.
- `ui.layer_shader(wgsl, { passes, images, geometry })`: a shader can declare passes that draw textures before each frame (persistent, in floats, with feedback and steps, or once), sample pictures, and read where tagged boxes are (`geometry = "tag"`), all by name. `zgx_window(p)` gives a shader window points. Rust gets the same as `FillShader::with_inputs`, and `ShaderInputs::views` feeds a shader from your own wgpu passes.
- **A native module moves and inserts rows of a shared list by key** (zgx-module, zgx-script): `Shared::upsert_at(key, rows)` gives each row a `Place` (just before a key's row, at the end, or where it is), so a module that diffs a reordered list on its own thread posts only the rows that moved or were made, not the whole list. One walk of the list; a row placed before itself, before a key no one has, or rows placed before one another are refused with nothing written. The host's `Shared` has the same, and `zgx_script::Place`. [The guide](docs/modules.md) says how.
- **A native module patches a shared list by key** (zgx-module, zgx-script): `Shared::upsert(key, rows)` replaces rows by a string field where they are and adds the new ones at the end, and `Shared::remove(key, keys)` removes them, so a module can diff its model on its own thread and post only the rows that changed. One pass over the list whatever the batch, the rows not given kept as they were. The host's `Shared` has the same, as `upsert` and `remove_rows`. [The guide](docs/modules.md) says how.
- **A span's text can fade in, drawn in Rust** (zgx, zgx-script): `ui.span { …, fade_in = { start = ui.now(), ms = 200, power = 1.6 } }` takes its text from clear to its colour, easing out. The paragraph recolours the run a frame at a time while it fades (`TextSpan::fade_in`, `TextFade`): no script runs for those frames and nothing lays out again, where a colour function reading `ui.now()` re-ran every span's bindings and rebuilt the paragraph's runs on every frame (a streamed reply fading in its new words ran script at the display's rate).
- **A span's icon can sit on a well of its own**: `ui.span { icon = { …, bg = "$well", radius = 4, pad = 2 } }` draws a rounded fill under the icon, `pad` past it on every side, over the span's own background, and the line keeps its room. A file chip's icon well, as Zeron's shipping app draws it, with nothing stacked. With no `bg`, nothing changes.
- **A span's padding can differ before and after its text**: `padding = { before, after, down }` (`x` and `{ across, down }` as before). A chip's icon close to its edge, its name with more room after it.
- **`focus_on_press = false`: a press on a node leaves focus where it is** (as the web's `mousedown` with `preventDefault()`): its clicks still come. A list hanging from a field takes clicks on its rows and tabs while the field keeps the keys and its caret.
- `ui.spring_to(state, target, opts)` runs a state to a value on a spring in Rust, with no script call per frame: for what a gesture hands over (its speed as `velocity`), with `from`, `delay`, `most` and `done`, and a `stop` that returns the speed it had. It rests within a thousandth of its range, so small ranges such as an opacity ease to their end.
- **Markdown tables say how their columns align** (zgx-script): a `table` block whose source aligns a column (`:--`, `:-:`, `--:`) has `align`, each column's `"start"`, `"center"`, `"end"`, or `""` where it says none. A table that aligns none has no `align`.
- **The terminal tells programs a cell's size in pixels** (zgx-terminal): `CSI 16 t` is answered with the cell's height and width, the size pictures are drawn by, as `CSI 14 t` already gave the text area's. A program sizing the images it sends by the cell, and asking rather than reading the pty's size (over ssh, where that size may not arrive), heard nothing.
- **The terminal reports its settings to DECRQSS** (zgx-terminal): a program asking for a setting (`DCS $ q`) is told the text's attributes (with underline styles as `4:3`), the cursor's style and the scrolling region as it would set them, and that any other isn't kept. nvim asks before drawing curly underlines, and draws them only once the answer has `4:3`; where `COLORTERM` isn't set, it turns on direct colour once the colour it set comes back.
- **The engine-drawn terminal clusters graphemes as Ghostty does, DEC mode 2027** (zgx-terminal; `zgx-alacritty-terminal` 0.26.3): an emoji ZWJ sequence (👨‍👩‍👧), a flag or a skin-toned emoji is one character in two cells, where it took six, four or more; VS15 makes an emoji one cell wide. The mode is on until a program resets it (`CSI ? 2027 l`), and DECRQM answers it: reset, each character takes its own width as `wcwidth` counts, so a shell's line editor keeps its cursor in step across ❤️.
- **The engine-drawn terminal shows pictures by the kitty graphics protocol** (zgx-terminal), as `kitten icat`, yazi, chafa and viu send them. Pictures come in the escape codes, whole or chunked, as RGB, RGBA or PNG, zlib-compressed or not. They are placed at the cursor over the cells they need, or stretched over the columns and rows asked for, under the text or over it. They scroll with their lines into the scrollback and go with it, on a clear or by `a=d`, with a 320 MB quota. Reading files or shared memory a program names is refused. A terminal that never shows a picture holds no room for one.
- **The engine-drawn terminal shows sixel images** (zgx-terminal), as chafa, lsix and img2sixel write them. They are placed at the cursor over the cells they need, and take those cells from pictures they cover wholly. The cursor then goes to the line of the image's last band, in its column, as xterm, foot and WezTerm put it. Device attributes now announce sixel (`CSI ? 62 ; 4 ; 22 c`, as foot answers), where they said a VT102.
- **A terminal draws underline styles and colours** (zgx-terminal): double, curly, dotted and dashed underlines (SGR 4:2 to 4:5) and underlines in a colour of their own (SGR 58), as Neovim and other editors mark diagnostics, where every underline was one line in the text's colour. They are shapes the GPU draws under the cells, a curly one a wave a cell long; a plain underline is still the text's own, and a row without these draws as before.
- **The terminal answers XTGETTCAP** (zgx-terminal): a program asking for terminfo capabilities by name (`DCS + q`) is told which this terminal has: direct colour (`RGB`, `Tc`, `setrgbf`, `setrgbb`), the clipboard through OSC 52 (`Ms`), the cursor's shape, styled and coloured underlines, the name and colours; one it hasn't is answered as not found. nvim asks for the direct colour ones where `COLORTERM` isn't set (over ssh, where it rarely arrives) and turns on its own colours once they're found.
- **The terminal answers XTVERSION** (zgx-terminal): a program asking for the terminal's name and version (`CSI > q`, as editors and prompts do where `TERM_PROGRAM` doesn't reach, over ssh or in tmux) is told `zgx(<version>)`, or the `TERM_PROGRAM` and `TERM_PROGRAM_VERSION` an app gives its session in `SessionOptions::env`.
- **`app:states()` and `app:retained_states()` in `zgx test`** (zgx-script): the first lists every named state's name in name order, the second how many States the scripts hold after a full collection, the count `zgx inspect gc --retained State` gives, so a test can assert a closed tab left none behind.
- **A textarea's lines can take room of their own from Luau** (zgx-script): `line_insets = function(line, state) return { left, above, below, hidden } end` gives a line an indent, space above and below, or hides it, from its text and the state `highlight` was given for it (a fenced block's lines know they're inside it). It's asked once for each line text and state; a textarea without it lays out as before.
- **The frame log names an effect no maker named by the Rust line that made it** (`zgx inspect frames`, `zgx::trace`): `zgx/src/compose.rs:812 effect` where it said `effect 124`, as #2257 did for writes. A binding's or a script's own name still wins. Only in `trace` builds (devtools, `inspect`): `Runtime::effect` and `effect_now` take their caller there alone.
- **An app places an inset title bar's traffic lights** (zgx-desktop, zgx-run, macOS): `WindowOptions::traffic_lights: Some((x, y))` puts the close button's top left there, in the content's points from the window's top left, as Electron's `trafficLightPosition`, so a toolbar taller or lower than AppKit's 40-point strip lines its controls up with them; `zgx.json` sets it as `"traffic_lights": { "x": 18, "y": 22 }`. They stay there through resizes, focus and appearance changes and full screen, and move with the window's zoom as the content does. `WindowInfo::controls` and `titlebar_area` (`ui.window.controls`) follow them, the strip as tall as centres them where they sit below AppKit's. Other title bars and platforms ignore it.
- **A test's `app:tree()` line says a toggle button is `pressed` or `not-pressed`**, as it says checked: a Luau test can assert it from the tree (`TreeLine::pressed` in Rust).
- **`ui.frames.measure(fn)` and `ui.frames.begin()` tell how smoothly an action's frames came** (zgx-script, zgx-dom): for analytics, counted as a web page's `requestAnimationFrame` measures count them, so the desktop's and the browser's numbers compare: `duration_ms` (the second frame), `blocked_ms` (gaps over 50 ms, whole), `settled_ms`, `frames`, `long_frames`, and each frame's time (`times`) to work out more. `begin` records a gesture until `finish`, 30 s at most. Nothing runs while no measure does.
- **`ui.map(state, { from = { a, b }, to = { c, d }, clamp })`** (zgx-script): a number mapped from a state in Rust, for a style that follows it with no script run as the state changes. A scrollbar's thumb follows a list's offset (`top = ui.map(offset, { from = { 0, limit }, to = { 4, finish }, clamp = true })`) with no script per frame. Each end is a number, a state or a function kept current as what it reads changes; a range of no width gives `c`. It sets numbers and lengths.
- **`ui.measure_text(text, style?)` gives the points a text takes, with nothing mounted** (zgx-script, zgx-dom): `{ w, h }` in a canvas text's font options (`size`, `font`, `weight`, `italic`, `tabular`, `line_height`, `letter_spacing`), wrapped at `max_w` if given. A table's column fits its widest cell this way. On the desktop it measures in the app's fonts, as `ui.render_canvas` does; in the browser, as the page lays the text out.
- `value_text` gives any element a value for assistive technology (`aria-valuetext`), not only a slider: a settings row reads its title as its name and its detail as its value.
- **3D scenes and glTF models in a video** (`zgx-video`): `ui.scene3d`, `ui.mesh`, `ui.light` and `ui.model` draw in a composition (the `3d` feature, on by default), and a video's frame shows the models it draws from its first, read before it is drawn as its pictures are decoded (`zgx::image::whole_frames_on_this_thread`), so a render in chunks or a still has them in every frame.
- **A video can draw its sound** (`zgx-video`): `video.audio_data(src)` gives a sound file's spectrum as bars (`song:bars { count }`), its loudness (`song:level()`) and its waveform (`song:waveform { count, span }`) at each frame, analysed in Rust and the same whichever frame came before.
- **Captions in a video** (`zgx-video`): `video.captions_of(src)` reads SubRip and WebVTT files, and `video.captions { src, words = true }` shows the caption of the moment, word by word if asked, the word being said highlighted.
- **Video files in a video** (`zgx-video`): `video.clip { src, from, trim, w, h }` shows the frame of the file each frame's time gives, decoded by ffmpeg, its sound mixed in. `zgx video render` takes `--dataset rows.json` to make one video per row, and `docs/video.md` describes it all.
- **Loops, freezes and volume curves in a video** (`zgx-video`): `video.loop { duration, times }` plays its children again each pass, their time and what starts as they mount starting again; `video.freeze { frame }` holds them at one frame; `video.audio { volume = function(frame) … end }` shapes a sound's loudness over its frames.
- **`zgx video render` writes H.264, HEVC and ProRes on macOS without ffmpeg** (zgx-video): where ffmpeg or its encoder is missing, macOS writes them itself (AVFoundation) instead of failing. The command now says which encoder writes each file and why. ffmpeg's encoders still write whenever they can, because they measured better at each quality (docs/video.md has the table). A render's sound, VP9, AV1 and video clips still need ffmpeg.
- **A composition plays inside any zgx app** (`zgx-video`): `zgx_video::Player` makes its frames on a thread of its own, as a render would, and `player.view()` shows them in a Rust view, or `player.expose(&host, name)` gives a Luau script a `host:<name>` image and actions to drive it. The studio is built on it.
- **The studio queues renders** (`zgx-video`): each render asked for waits its turn, shows its progress and result, and can be stopped while queued or running.
- **Stills and thumbnails of a video** (`zgx-video`): `zgx_video::render_still(&options, frame, max_width)` draws one frame as a render would, with nothing encoded, and `zgx video still --thumbnail WIDTH` writes one; `--every-nth-frame N` keeps one frame in N.
- **WebM with alpha, and AV1** (`zgx-video`): `zgx video render … out.webm` writes VP9 with its alpha kept and Opus sound, for a page to lay over its own content; `--codec av1` writes AV1 in an MP4.
- **Programmatic video** (`zgx-video`, new and optional, from the repository: not on crates.io yet, and an app using it as a library takes every zgx crate from the same revision; `zgx::clock`): a composition is an ordinary zgx interface, in Luau (`require("@video")`: `video.composition`, `video.frame`, `video.sequence`, `video.series`, `video.interpolate`, `video.spring`, transitions and `video.audio`), and `zgx video render <dir> out.mp4` draws it offscreen a frame at a time, no window and no browser, to H.264, HEVC, ProRes 4444 with alpha (by ffmpeg), a PNG for each frame or a GIF. `zgx::clock::VirtualClock` gives a thread its own time, which animations, `ui.after`, `ui.every`, shader clocks, GIFs, `ui.now`, `os.clock` and `math.random` follow there, so the same input makes the same frames. An app not using it reads one more flag where it read the clock.
- **`visible_margin` on `on_visible`** (zgx-script, `NodeHandle::observe_visibility_within` in zgx): an element counts as in view once it comes within that many points of showing, as an IntersectionObserver's `rootMargin` does. It's judged natively as the view scrolls, and calls `on_visible` only as an element crosses, so a script can build what is about to scroll in without reading the scroll position every frame.
- **`WGPU_FORCE_FALLBACK_ADAPTER=1` draws on WARP even beside a GPU** (zgx-gpu, Windows): the renderer's device, Direct3D 12's and Direct3D 11's, is on Windows' software adapter, as on a machine without a GPU, such as CI's runners. A machine with a GPU can then run the tests CI runs on WARP and bless software goldens. Unset, the adapter is chosen as before: the first GPU DXGI ranks for low power. On Linux it takes a software Vulkan driver; macOS has no software Metal adapter, so there the renderer fails to start with it set.
- **A web view can forget a site** (zgx-script, zgx-desktop): `web:clear_site_data(url)`, a promise of how many records went, removes everything the page's profile keeps for the url's site, its registrable domain and the subdomains under it (cookies, local and session storage, IndexedDB, caches, service workers), for every web view on that profile, as a browser's "Remove site data" does: an app's "Forget login for this site". Pages open keep what they hold in memory until they reload. macOS and Linux remove WebKit's own records for the site; Windows clears the host's origins and the cookies a request to it carries, so storage of the site's other subdomains, and cookies one set for itself alone, stay there.
- **WebP images** (zgx-gpu, zgx-desktop, zgx-script): `ui.image` and the decoders read lossy and lossless WebP (`image`'s pure-Rust decoder; an animated file shows its first frame), kept as drawn like PNG and JPEG and shrunk to the size they are drawn at. A WebP decodes whole before it is shrunk (it has no scaled decode), so a big photo peaks at its full size for a moment. New `webp` feature, on by default, in zgx-gpu, zgx-desktop and zgx-script.
- **A mouse wheel can move a `scroll_x`** (zgx, zgx-script, zgx-desktop): `View::wheel_across`, Luau `wheel_across = true`. A wheel has no sideways axis, so with Shift it moves the view (and never the page), and without it moves the view once the pointer has been moved onto it, going on to the views around it after a pause at the view's edge. A view that slid under a resting pointer leaves the wheel to the page. Windows and Linux report Shift and the wheel as vertical; a Mac reports it as sideways, which every `scroll_x` already took. A trackpad's swipe mostly down leaves such a view still.
- **A window's glass, frame and level from `zgx.json` and `ui.window`, by platform** (zgx-script, zgx-run): `glass` takes a material and `plain` (`{ "material": "under_window_background", "plain": true }`), beside `true`; `decorations`, `resizable`, `transparent`, `shadow`, `covers_display` and `level` (`"always_on_top"`) are new keys of both, asked of the platform as a Rust host's `WindowOptions` asks them. `"macos"`, `"windows"` and `"linux"` blocks replace a window's keys on that platform, key by key (a table such as `glass` whole); a block holds window keys only, and every block is checked on every platform. `ui.window`'s types and the lint know them all (the lint no longer warns on `visible` or the size limits). [Native](docs/reference/native.md#windows) lists them.
- **`ui.window.popups` lists the open menus, popovers and tooltips** (zgx, zgx-script): a read-only state of `{ x, y, w, h, kind }` in window points (kind `"menu"`, `"popover"`, `"tooltip"`), following as they open, close and move, for placing something outside open menus.
- **`WindowCapabilities::blur_pass` and `blur_lift`** (zgx-desktop): how much of what is behind the window its system blur lets through, and what it adds. Windows' acrylic lets 83% through and adds 8. macOS and Linux report a pure blur. An app can use them to pick a scrim that comes out the same on every platform.
- **Scrollbars on Windows show arrow buttons as they widen** (zgx): as WinUI's do, 12 long at each end (always, where scrollbars always show), a press stepping the view as an arrow key does, their carets in the thumb's colour; the thumb travels between them. macOS and Linux have none.
- **A Windows scrollbar's arrow buttons repeat while held, and wash under the pointer** (zgx): as WinUI's RepeatButtons, a step at once, the next after 500 ms, then one each 50 ms until let go, each a single wake; under the pointer a button washes (the text at 5%) and its caret takes the secondary text's colour.
- **An engine the app pulls blocks from** (zgx-audio): `Audio::pulled(config, wake)` gives an engine with no thread of its own, and `Pull::fill(out)` mixes each block asked for. It's what a browser's audio worklet drives. Streams decode inline as blocks are filled. Nothing is asked for before the first sound, which calls `wake`, or after the quiet hold, when `fill` says to stop. It sounds as an offline engine does, sample for sample. zgx-audio builds for wasm32.
- **zgx-audio, a crate for sound** (zgx-audio): a mixer that places every play, stop and parameter change on an exact frame, whatever the device's block size. It has voices with gain, pan, pitch and a low-pass ramped a sample at a time, sends, buses in a tree with gain, mute, solo and fades, effects on buses (a compressor, a ducker, a reverb, an echo, filters and an EQ band, or an app's own through the `Effect` trait), and a look-ahead limiter on master. It reads WAV files, and streams or loads Ogg Vorbis, MP3, Opus and (on macOS) AAC behind features of their own. It has spatial maths (fall-off, cones, Doppler, panning), binaural output (`hrtf`), an offline renderer, and the system's default output (`device`). Whatever comes in, only finite samples come out. It has no default features, and zgx's crates reach it only through their own `audio` features, so apps that don't use sound link none of it.
- **zgx-dom under a strict Content-Security-Policy** (`zgx-dom`): its classes go in a constructed style sheet the document adopts, so a page whose `style-src` blocks inline `<style>` still renders styled. Browsers that can't adopt a sheet keep a `<style>` element.
- **Popover arrows in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): a popover's `arrow` points at its anchor's centre from the side facing it, in the panel's own background, flipping with the panel.
- **Canvas pictures, shadows and radial and conic gradients in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): a canvas's `Draw::Image` (fitted in its box, cut to its corners, at its opacity), `Draw::Shadow` (offset, blurred, grown or shrunk by its spread, the shape itself not drawn) and `Brush::Radial` and `Brush::Conic` fills and strokes draw with Canvas 2D as on the desktop.
- **Canvas drawing in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): `canvas` draws its commands with Canvas 2D at the element's size and the screen's pixel ratio (fills, strokes with caps, joins and dashes, gradients and stripes, clips, text), again as its size or what it reads changes, and only when the commands differ.
- **The clipboard in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): zgx-dom provides zgx's `Platform` around every view it mounts, so `PlatformService` and Luau's `ui.clipboard` read and write the browser's clipboard, text and HTML both ways. A key a keymap binds to paste reads what is pasted, and `open_url` opens a tab.
- **Context menus in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): `View::context_menu`, `context_menu_with_span` and `TextSpan::context_menu` open their entries as a menu at the pointer on a secondary click, replacing the browser's own; with no entries, the browser's shows.
- **Drags in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): a drag a listener starts (`on_drag`, `start_drag`) moves a copy of its source with the pointer, and `on_drop` destinations take it as on the desktop; its source hears how it ended (`on_drag_end`).
- **Listeners, keymaps and focus groups in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): `on_event` (and `on_action`) listeners hear the browser's pointer, wheel, key, focus and activation events in their capture, target and bubble phases, their decisions applied to the event at once. Keymaps resolve keys with zgx's own resolution (contexts, sequences) before anything hears them and route their actions to listeners. Focus groups, list boxes, tab lists, toolbars and radio groups are one Tab stop moved by their arrows.
- **Lazy overlays in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): a `lazy` modal, popover or menu builds what's in it the first time it opens, as on the desktop, and tooltips are lazy, so a page of them costs no nodes until one shows.
- **Luau apps in the browser** (`zgx-dom`'s `luau` feature): zgx-script runs a Luau app in the browser and zgx-dom mounts its view as DOM, its state bound to the nodes that show it. Luau's VM is built for wasm32 with wasi-sdk (`crates/zgx-dom/luau-env.sh`), and zgx-script no longer links the renderer there. Images, `ui.fetch`, sockets, plugins and hot reload don't run in the browser yet ([docs/dom.md](docs/dom.md#luau-in-the-browser)).
- **`canvas::measure_text` in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): while a canvas draws in the browser, it measures text with Canvas 2D in the font the text will draw in, so labels can be placed around each other as on the desktop.
- **Overlays in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): `modal` is a `<dialog>` shown with `showModal()`; `popover`, `menu`, `submenu` and tooltips are popovers in the browser's top layer, placed against their anchors. Escape, a click outside and focus behave as the browser's own, and their closing sets the view's `open` signal. Menus move among their items with the arrows and open submenus with Right or the pointer; choosing an item closes its menus before its click runs. The same view code as on the desktop.
- **Rich text in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): `rich_text` and `rich_text_signal` paragraphs show as text nodes, styled `<span>`s and `<a>` links, their looks shared classes. A web or mail link opens in a tab of its own unless its click handled it, hover styles are `:hover` rules, icons masked boxes, and `fade_in` a CSS animation. Spans that look as they did keep their nodes, so streamed text only appends.
- **Variable and measured virtual lists in the DOM** (`zgx-dom`): `variable_virtual_list`, `measured_virtual_list` and `measured_rows` keep only the rows in view, each at its own height; measured rows report the height they lay out at (ResizeObserver). As heights above the view change, the first row in view stays where it shows, or the end for a list anchored there.
- **zgx views as browser DOM** (`zgx-dom`, new, wasm32): a Rust-composed view mounts into real DOM elements, laid out, shaped, scrolled and read to assistive technology by the browser, with each signal write touching only the nodes that read it: no virtual DOM. Updates reach JS once per frame as an operation buffer; static styles become shared classes, and hover, pressed and focus styles CSS rules; keyed rows move the fewest nodes and repeated rows are stamped from templates. Luau and GPU content don't run in it yet ([docs/dom.md](docs/dom.md)). zgx's clock reads the browser's on wasm32.
- **`zgx-fx`, an optional package of visual effects**: glow, pulse, ripple, wave, shimmer, sparkles, aurora, grain, electric, fire, motion blur; heat haze, shockwave, lens, clear glass, CRT, chromatic split, fluted glass over what's behind a node; liquid metal, mesh gradient, dither, grain gradient, metaballs, swirl, voronoi, waves, halftone and plastic fills. Rust: `view.fx(Glow::neon())`. Luau: copy `luau/fx.luau`, `require("fx")`. Made only with zgx's public API: an app that doesn't depend on it links none of it. Not on crates.io yet: use it from the repository, with every zgx crate from the same revision (one from crates.io beside it would be a second zgx); `luau/fx.luau` needs no crate.
- **`ZGX_OFFSCREEN=1`** (zgx-desktop, macOS): test and probe sessions keep their windows off anyone's screen. No window is ever ordered in: made hidden, a show, focus, restore, fullscreen or zoom of one does nothing, popups stay inside their window, and no native menu, prompt, file panel, web alert, look-up, notification or browser shows; WindowServer lists none of its windows on any screen. Its windows are also invisible and click-through, out of Mission Control and window cycling, and the app takes no Dock icon or Cmd-Tab place and never activates. It goes on drawing as the app has its windows shown (it implies `ZGX_RENDER_WHEN_COVERED`) and answering `zgx inspect`, whose snapshots are zgx's own frames.
- **`zgx run` builds an app's native modules only when they change, and `zgx build` writes a bundle** (zgx-script, zgx-run): zgx-run loads the modules an app's `zgx.json` names (`"modules": { "words": "modules/words" }`) before its scripts. `zgx run my-app` builds each one whose sources changed (keyed by a hash of them and the compiler, cached for every app on the machine), and as one changes builds it again and restarts the app, saying so in its banner. `zgx build my-app` writes `dist/<app_id>/`: the scripts compiled to bytecode, each module's library for each `--target` (from the cache unless its sources changed) and a manifest of each file's hash, which `zgx-run dist/my-app` runs, checking each module's library as it opens and each file as it is read. A release where only Luau or a module changed compiles no app. [The guide](docs/modules.md) says how.
- **`zgx.json` has a JSON Schema** (zgx-run): `crates/zgx-run/zgx.schema.json`, made from the same key tables zgx-run reads (a test keeps it so), so an editor completes and checks a `zgx.json` that names it with `"$schema"`, which zgx.json now takes: its window keys, look and platform blocks, its named windows and `first`, and its packages, plugins, modules and the rest.

### Performance

- **A change inside an absolute box lays out that box alone** (zgx): a scrollbar's thumb moved in its rail, a badge, a menu's item no longer lay out every ancestor to the window's root and their other children each frame. The walk up from a change stops at the parent of the first absolute node it passes (placed by that parent, not by a `relative` block above), which is laid out again alone and stands if it answers its parent as it did; otherwise layout runs as before. A thumb in a fixed rail went from 36 measures a frame to 6, the same however long the page.
- **An accessibility update looks only at what changed.** With a screen reader or another assistive client attached, each update used to walk the whole tree. Now text or a move looks at those nodes alone, so what a client adds to a streamed chunk no longer grows with the tree. A flush that changes structure still walks the whole tree, and clients get the same updates as before.
- **A frame of an animation or a tween runs none of the node's style functions** (zgx-script): animating a layout or paint property (`anime.animate(el, { w = … })`), or a `ui.tween` or state beside them, resolved all the node's styles again each frame, so each `bg = function() … end` and the like ran in Luau on every frame though nothing it read had changed. From the first time that would happen, a node's functions resolve apart, when what they read changes.
- **Text measured without a shaper reads an ASCII text's graphemes from its bytes** (zgx): each byte is a grapheme but a carriage return before a line feed, so `FallbackTextLayout` no longer runs Unicode's segmentation over ASCII text. A headless host (tests, offscreen hosts) measures text 6% faster in whole runs: script-update −6.6%, mount −6.1%, with the same sizes and carets.
- **A screen reader's text costs a third less to send** (zgx, zgx-gpu, zgx-desktop): placing carets on a text's lines and building a screen reader's text runs walked every grapheme through Unicode's segmentation, three times a character for carets. An ASCII text's graphemes now come from its bytes (`zgx::text_layout::grapheme_ranges`), so a full accessibility update of a transcript costs 32.6% less, with the same runs and carets.
- **Text shapes an ASCII word without looking up each character's script** (zgx-cosmic-text): an ASCII run's script is Latin if it has a letter and none otherwise, so it's read from its bytes rather than guessed one character at a time. A dashboard whose values change every frame flushes 0.78% faster (heavy-both, 1,120 → 1,111 K instructions a frame), drawing exactly as before.
- **A filling glyph atlas gives back only the glyphs no text holds** (zgx-gpu): the cells of glyphs no text the renderer holds any more are freed where they lie, and the rest stay where they are, so the text on screen keeps its glyphs and its placed quads; before, every fill started the atlas over, rasterizing and placing every visible glyph again (three quarters of that cost was the placing). The atlas packs cells in shelves that give cells back one at a time. Archiving a message in the mail example costs 9.6% less (paint 3.62 to 2.75 M instructions, 798 to 531 glyphs rasterized), and an atlas that fills again soon still grows as before.
- **A glyph atlas grows without drawing its glyphs again** (zgx-gpu): growing copies the atlas into the larger one's corner and scales the texture coordinates of the quads already placed from it by the power of two between the sizes, which is exact, so they read as placing them fresh would. Before, growing rasterized every glyph on screen and placed every text's quads again. Archiving a message in the mail example, where the atlas grows, costs 22% less (paint 3.62 to 1.62 M instructions, 798 to 168 glyphs rasterized).
- **A window back from a long idle draws its text from cache, with no hitch** (zgx-gpu): the glyph atlas a window grew no longer starts over empty at rest. It shrinks to the smallest atlas holding the glyphs and icons on screen, copied across rather than rasterized again, and an atlas the screen fills stays. On an M5, a page of CJK text whose atlas had grown to 16 MiB rests at 4 MiB (the screen's 600 glyphs), and the first whole repaint after the idle draws in 0.25 ms of paint instead of 9 ms (114–126M instructions to 0.6M).
- **A fade's bands are worked out once a frame** (zgx-gpu): a frame drawing a fade's bands as one worked each run's strips out twice, first to find whether any run needs them and again to draw it, over every item of the run (a list's hundreds of rows under an edge fade). The first run found keeps its strips for drawing, and the runs before it, found to need none, aren't asked again. Scrolling a deep transcript under glass and a fade runs 3.4% fewer instructions.
- **A binding is built where it is kept** (zgx): `Ui::bind` makes its callback's place first and builds the callback in it, where `Rc::new` moved what it holds (a node's styles and inputs, hundreds of bytes) once more, for every styled node a mount makes. The mount benchmark does 0.49% fewer instructions, mounting text 0.34%.
- **A frosted panel holds 32 MB less on macOS, and its blur's first pass takes half the GPU time** (zgx-gpu): a backdrop blur wide enough to average its area down by 8 did so with 16 samples in flight per pixel, which held so many registers that Metal's driver kept four 8 MB blocks of scratch for as long as the window drew. It now averages down in two halves of four samples (the first into a texture at twice the slot's size), on every backend. A pixel of the blur may differ by 1 in 255 from before.
- **A frosted panel costs the GPU a fifth less a frame**: its backdrop blur's reduce pass took each texel's samples in a loop of a count known only at run time, each waiting on the last. They are now written out for each factor, the same samples in the same order, so the pixels are the same to the byte: a 1280 by 110 frosted bar over a scrolling list, 661 to 538 µs of GPU a frame at 2x, 1,338 to 1,099 at 3x (M5, Metal).
- **A window with backdrop blur walks only what a frame's damage reaches** (zgx-gpu, zgx): a frame of a window with any backdrop blur (frosted glass) walked every node of the window, wherever its damage was, so a row sliding in a sidebar paid for the whole window each frame. The damage is now grown through every filter it could touch first (`Scene::backdrop_filters`) and the walk kept within that, which holds everything the frame draws: the same items, in the same order, and the same pixels. In Zeron's sidebar, a chat's message (its rows sliding, then a working session's glyph turning) costs 53% less drawing on the main thread, 17% less of the process's CPU.
- **A canvas's line keeps its font uncopied** (zgx): a line drawn again in the font it had is compared with the node's in place, and its font is copied (its family, features and fallbacks each a shared count) only when it changed, and compared once. A terminal's log streaming in takes 0.2% fewer instructions.
- **A canvas's text keeps no copy of each line** (zgx): a canvas's labels compare each line with the last drawing's, which the canvas's node holds anyway, instead of keeping a clone of every line (its text and font, four shared counts each) to compare with. A terminal's changed run costs about seven fewer atomic counts; a log streaming in takes 0.5% fewer instructions, and each canvas holds one drawing where it held a copy of every line.
- **A canvas's changed lines work out their group's place once** (zgx): each changed line of a canvas's text (a terminal's runs) found its group's translation and clip again, walking every ancestor and summing each clipping one's offset. A run of changed lines now shares it, worked out by the first. A log streaming into a terminal takes 1% fewer instructions; nothing else runs new code.
- **A rasterized canvas pans without rasterizing** (zgx-gpu): a `ui.canvas` with a `view` whose drawing rasterizes (curves, clips, gradients, shadows: a design board's) rasterized all of its box again on every pan step. Its raster now moves with the view in whole device pixels, and a pan onto more of the drawing rasterizes only that part, kept beside the rest up to the 32 MiB raster budget; a zoom rasterizes what shows, as before. A 328-item design board at 1.5x went from 84 ms of rasterizing per pan step to 0.01 ms (p95), with an occasional step of a few milliseconds where a pan first shows a part.
- **A canvas that pans and zooms draws its text into its raster** (zgx, zgx-gpu): a `ui.canvas` with a `view` kept every text as a text node over its raster, so each zoom step laid out and drew every word again, at the new size, on the frame, and text could show stale after a zoom. Its text is now drawn into its raster with its shapes, in their layers, cut by its clips and turned by its transforms (as `ui.render_canvas` draws it), and pans and zooms as pixels with the rest (#2100, #2101). On a design board with 323 texts, a zoom step went from 63 to 247 ms to 12 to 21 ms, and a frame of a panel resize from 29 to 8 ms (debug build).
- **A rasterized canvas zooms without rasterizing on the frame** (zgx-gpu): a `ui.canvas` with a `view` whose drawing rasterizes rasterized its box again on each zoom step, on the frame. In a window, a zoom step now shows the raster of the zoom before, scaled by the GPU, while the new zoom's is made on a thread of its own; the window is woken and draws it once made. One is made at a time per canvas, for the latest zoom. A design board's zoom step went from 70 to 82 ms on the frame (p95) to under 1 ms, sharp again 65 to 85 ms later. Offscreen renderers still rasterize on the frame.
- **A column changed at its end places only its end** (zgx): a column that resumed at its end sets aside the children it kept while it is arranged, so they aren't walked again, and takes them back as the layout ends; if it moved, it is arranged whole. Every box is where a whole layout puts it; debug builds check each stands where it is placed.
- **A layout with no column streamed into makes no call for one** (zgx): a layout pass looks at whether columns resumed at their end set children aside, or are streamed into at all, before calling what returns or restores them, so a scene with neither pays a test, not two calls a pass.
- **A column streamed into lays out only its end** (zgx): a column whose height is its children's, once its children outgrow their list past sixty-four, looks first at whether only its end changed (its last block grown, or blocks appended): its earlier children's sizes and places from its last result stand, its sums and what it reads resume where its end begins, and only the end is measured. Every other node's layout is as it was. Every box is where a whole layout puts it; debug builds check what it keeps against asking every child.
- **A streamed column's layout costs the same however long the reply** (zgx): a column resuming at its end tells its kept children's places stand from the child at its end's place, not by comparing every child's id, and a reorder forgets what it kept. Debug builds still check what it keeps against every child, but not while phases are counted.
- **What `compile_ahead` holds and hashes is lighter** (zgx-script): a module compiled ahead is let go as soon as its `require` has loaded it, not held to the end of the load. Each file's cache key is made on the worker, not on the main thread as the load takes it. A key hashes at most a source's first and last 64 KB, and a hit is still compared byte for byte. The worker finds a file's `require`s with a substring search, not a seven-byte comparison at every byte.
- **A scroll frame decides whether to copy its pixels from what lies around its content** (zgx, zgx-gpu): when nothing the content holds paints otherwise than a plain item does (no layer, backdrop blur, fade of its own, dithered picture, layer shader, baked loop or raised child), its own paint items are no longer made each frame only to be checked. `Scene::paints_plainly` looks at its nodes as far down as the paint walk would go, and `Scene::layer_items_around` walks the window leaving out what the content holds. Anything else, or content that might cover what lies under it, takes the whole walk as before; debug builds have both decide and compare. Scrolling an expanded 1,000-call tool group in Zeron, paint goes from 274.6M to 255.0M instructions a second (−7.1%), copying as before.
- **A scroll frame decides whether to copy its pixels without walking up the tree for each item** (zgx-gpu): what it asks of each paint item in its content, whether the item lies within the scroll view, is answered from the nodes between the view and the item before it, since in paint order an item's parent is one of them. Before, each of the 300 items in a transcript climbed the 16 nodes to the scroll view. Scrolling an expanded 1,000-call tool group in Zeron, `copy_blocker` costs 280K instructions a frame instead of 362K, and paint 2,270K instead of 2,350K (−3.5%), copying the same pixels.
- **A scroll under a floating frosted panel copies its pixels** (zgx-gpu): a backdrop-blurred panel over a scroll view that doesn't repaint (a composer over a transcript, its shadow reaching into the view) no longer makes every frame repaint the whole view and blur it in full. Its blur reads only within its own box, so it repaints where it is and where the copy moved it, blurring what moved under it afresh, and the rest of the view is copied. Layers over the view are carried the same way; only one reading what lies under it (a blend, a shader reading the backdrop) still repaints the view.
- **Text laid out without an event loop keeps nothing Core Text autoreleased** (zgx-text, macOS): looking up the face for a character the text's fonts lack hands back objects Core Text autoreleases, which went only when an autorelease pool drained. An app's event loop drains one each turn, but text laid out without one (a headless renderer, a thread of its own) kept them: some 9 MB at rest after a page of new Chinese characters. Each lookup now drains its own pool.
- **A window at rest gives back the glyphs Core Text drew** (zgx-gpu, zgx-desktop, macOS): the fonts zgx made to draw faces swash can't (macOS 26's PingFang) were kept for good, and Core Text keeps each font's glyphs while it lives. After a page of Chinese text read through at three sizes that was some 30 MB of heap, 40 MB of footprint, at rest. The idle trim now lets them go, with the GPU renderer and the software raster alike; the next glyph drawn makes its font again.
- With Direct3D 11 (`ZGX_D3D11`), a grown glyph atlas shrinks at idle by copying its glyphs across, as with Direct3D 12, instead of starting over: the first frame after a long idle no longer rasterizes the screen's text again.
- **Direct3D 11 backdrop readers apart share one copy** (zgx-gpu, `d3d11`): layer shaders reading the backdrop that needn't see each other read one copy of the target, as on Direct3D 12, instead of a copy each.
- **Damage is kept by its edges**: a rectangle joining a frame's damage compares with each one held, and worked out each one's far edges to do it. They're now kept, so a frame that damages many places scans for less: `animate` −1.7%, `style` −0.8%, `anime` −0.8%, on arm64. The damage itself is the same, to the bit.
- **The image decoding thread gives its heap back once its copies are** (zgx, zgx-desktop): decoded copies are made on that thread and given back by the main one at rest, and mimalloc returns a block another thread freed only when its own thread collects. `zgx::image::set_decoder_rest` names what that thread runs then; zgx-desktop collects its heap. A heavy list on the iOS simulator rested 12 MB lower after a fling; 180 photo decodes on macOS, 2.5 MB lower. Nothing runs as pictures decode or draw.
- **A list's rows are stamped without re-reading their shape** (zgx-dom): each row is checked against the shape the last one was stamped from, part by part as it's read, and is stamped from that template directly when it matches, with no key built, hashed or looked up. Creating 10,000 rows of the table benchmark takes 2% less time, and its script 7% less.
- **A list growing at its end looks at its new rows alone** (zgx, zgx-script): a `ui.each` over a list that keeps its first items (a streamed reply's blocks, its last one growing) matches only the items after them to rows, and `keyed` mounts, removes and hashes only the keys after an unchanged prefix, where that is well under the whole list. Each chunk of a streamed Markdown reply had hashed every block's key again: the `md-stream` cell (400 sections) does 24.4% fewer instructions, with the same rows, order and reuse.
- **A fade drawn as one costs less a frame** (zgx-gpu): each frame that draws a fade's bands as one looked through the whole run of items under it for ids to key its textures (a lookup per item, hundreds in a transcript), and worked out each item's reach three times. It now looks only as far as the textures need, works each reach out once, and clones only the items a by-item strip cuts. What is drawn is the same.
- **Fade bands size what they build once** (zgx-gpu): a run of items under one fade mask (a list's rows under an edge fade) was gathered push by push, and so were its band's items, each growing about ten times a frame for hundreds of rows; each is now made at its length, and a run's texture keys are gathered only for a run with bands to draw. Banding is worked out out of line, so a frame with nothing faded runs the code it ran. A scrolled frame under a frosted, faded panel reallocates 28 times fewer, and scroll-glass runs 1.9% fewer instructions.
- **A flex row that narrows past a sibling's minimum measures its children once** (zgx): a round of flex distribution that stopped one child at its floor shared the room out again, but had already measured the others at the share it gave them, and threw that away. A wrapping text beside a bullet or an icon was measured at three widths a frame while a split was dragged; it is now measured at two. Layout is unchanged: a child whose padding and border are wider than its share still runs the rounds as before.
- **A folded row holds less until it opens, and a list's estimate frees what it read at idle** (zgx-script): a `Value::folded` row (a long transcript's) kept room for what it would open to, and an estimate reading it left that room holding the opened table's allocation. That room is now made only once a script or a host's `field` opens the row; an estimate's reading leaves nothing behind, and what it opened goes at the next idle rather than a free a row in the pass. 15,000 folded rows hold 20.7 MB rather than 21.3 at rest, 22.0 rather than 24.5 under an estimate, whose first pass costs 168M instructions rather than 183M.
- **A frame's delivery no longer allocates or hashes with SipHash** (zgx): `FrameClock::deliver` worked out each rate's answer in a map made every refresh; it now keeps the handful there are in a list on the stack. The executor's task map and wake set, hashed at every wake, and the clock's cadence use Fx, as task ids and divisors are small integers.
- **Frames paced by the timer no longer skip or repeat a refresh** (zgx-desktop, macOS): the next frame's deadline is the refresh after the last one delivered, not the first after the moment it's set. With the timer's grid a little off the display's, a frame ending just past a refresh's start used to skip it, and a timer waking just before one delivered the last refresh again: on a long list scrolled by the wheel, 412 skips and 363 repeats in 43,000 refreshes became 64 and 1, and refreshes missed for it about 70% fewer. More than half a refresh behind, a frame still skips to the next rather than catching up.
- **A long column's paint bound is updated, not recomputed, as its last rows grow**: each chunk of a streamed reply worked out its column's ink over every block it held. A plain container of 64 children or more now keeps the union of all but its last 8, works those out each time, and takes in only those of the rest that grew where they were; anything else is worked out over every child as before.
- **A pointer move hit tests once** (zgx): its dispatch and its cursor asked the scene the same, each walking it; the last answer is kept with the revisions it was found at.
- **A pointer moving beside a list of rows with icons no longer walks every row**: the hit test skips a subtree the point is outside once nothing in it can be hit past that side, and an image or SVG a view mounts drawn in its box (an icon) no longer stops that proof; one transformed past its box, or appended to the scene directly, still does. A sidebar of 500 rows with icons: 64 nodes walked a move instead of 2,064.
- **A script showing a video on macOS draws its frames as they are** (zgx-script, zgx-video): `ui.image { src = "host:<name>" }` shows a frame the app sets with the new `ScriptHost::surface` from its own storage, and a player exposed to a script (`Player::expose`) sets its pixel buffers so, where it read every frame back to bytes and uploaded them again. Its bytes are read only if something asks (`bg_image`, `dither`, `characters`, or the script). A player replaced under its name now stops, where it played on unseen.
- **Content scrolling under a still pointer finds the row under it without walking every row's parts** (zgx): the hit test that keeps hover right each scroll frame skips a row the pointer isn't over by the proof its box keeps of which sides its subtree reaches past, wherever the scroll has moved it. Before, the proof was used only for nodes at rest in a scene without raised or sticky children or popups, so a scroll's rows were all entered. In a list of 1,000 five-node rows, a scroll frame's hit test visits at most 1,016 nodes instead of 6,004, with or without a raised node in the window.
- **Hover over a long scrolled list finds the row under the pointer by search** (zgx): a plain hit test over a node of 64 children or more, whose boxes run down in order with none transformed, now looks only at the children reaching the pointer's height. The others are skipped because each proves nothing of it reaches past its top or bottom, checked each frame. A frame that scrolls rows under a still pointer runs in 95K instructions at 2,000 rows instead of 278K, and in 341K at 8,000 instead of 1,057K, and finds the same nodes.
- **An icon mounts in the frame's one layout pass** (zgx): an SVG view's artwork fills its box from the start, rather than taking its box's size once laid out and asking for a second pass; a row of icons lays out as a row of plain boxes does. An icon's artwork also stays in its own box under a `relative` ancestor (an image's or a canvas's drawing too).
- **A photo shrunk to the size it's drawn takes a third of the time** (zgx, zgx-gpu): `resample::shrink` takes its area mean down each target row's few source rows and then across, a fixed few taps each way with each axis's weights summing to one, instead of over the whole area with a division a texel. It's within 1/255 of what it made before. A 1600 by 1200 JPEG drawn 1083 by 812 shrinks in 2.9 ms, not 8.3 (thread CPU time, M5 Max), so a fast scroll through photos shows each one sooner. The decoding thread premultiplies sixteen pixels at a time, passing over opaque blocks (every JPEG's), as the renderer does: the two share `zgx::image::premultiply`.
- **An image uploads in fewer instructions, a video frame's most of all** (zgx-gpu): premultiplying a straight image's pixels goes sixteen at a time, leaving a block all opaque (a photo's, a video's) as it is and making one all transparent (around a canvas's marks) zeros, and computes the rest exactly with a multiply and shifts in 16 bits, vectorized, where it divided each channel a pixel at a time; red and blue swap a pixel a word as the texels go to Metal. The pixels are the same (every colour and alpha checked). A 1080p clip playing in an app's window went from 2.67 G to 2.04 G instructions a second.
- **Pictures past the image budget upload and decode once, however slowly they decode** (zgx, zgx-gpu): pictures still decoding now count towards the budget's shares, so the others no longer look as if they fit and upload whole, only to go again a frame later. Three 5000×2500 kept files took 13 uploads, three of them 50 MB textures, and 18 to 45 when decoding was slow; they now take 3, at any speed. A decode a larger one has replaced no longer runs, nor is that larger one queued twice.
- **A mount draws its text inputs from one layout** (zgx): each deferred draw laid the scene out again for its own box, so a mount of n inputs ran n layout passes; now it lays out once for all of them, and a draw whose box another moved is drawn again. A layout pass with many boundaries to lay out (one an input) sorts them by one number.
- **Typing and clicking fast keep the display's full rate** (zgx-desktop, macOS): a frame answering a press, a key or text draws at once and shows as soon as it's ready, as a scroll's does. Frames ready near their refresh point used to keep the every-other-refresh cadence of the frames before them, so a dashboard re-rendering on each input showed 60 frames a second on a 120 Hz display; it now shows about 117. Frames an app's own timers make keep that cadence, which keeps a stream from alternating between one refresh and the next.
- **A fixed-size absolute box moved by its inset alone measures nothing** (zgx): a scrollbar's thumb whose `top` follows a scroll offset is placed where its parent's layout would put it, and arranged alone, with no layout of its rail or anything else. The move is read from its parent's last layout (the style that layout mapped for it) and taken only if those insets give where it is now; otherwise its walk is as before.
- **`ui.json.decode` reads a document straight into tables** (zgx-script): it was parsed into zgx's values, those made into tables, then dropped; it is now read onto a flat tape (each value a token in order, its strings' bytes in one buffer) and the tables made from that in one call. A 10,000-message feed decodes in 34-36 ms, not 47-54 (headless, M5 Max), and the same tables come out. An object naming a key twice keeps its last value, as before, and counts the key twice in `values_converted`.
- **JSON is read straight into script values** (zgx-script): `ui.json.decode`, `Value::from_json` and JSON replies no longer build serde_json's tree and then a copy of it, and each object key is one string however many objects name it. A 6.8 MB feed of 10,000 messages reads in 282M instructions where it took 685M, and drops in 92M where it took 150M. `Value::from_json` no longer parses its text twice.
- **`ui.json.decode` makes its tables in a third of the time** (zgx-script): a tree of lists and maps a script is given (a decoded JSON document, a state read whole) is made into Luau tables in one call through Luau's C API, where a map holding a list (a feed's message) set each field through mlua, a closure made and a protected call each. A 10,000-message feed's tables took 65-76 ms and take 24-32 (headless, M5 Max), and its first frame comes 50 ms sooner. The same tables, counted the same.
- **A long list paused mid-scroll no longer stalls a frame keeping it** (zgx, zgx-gpu): a frame that moves nothing after a scroll (a pause, a reversal) is kept to patch, and keeping walked and laid out every item in the window, off-screen rows included: 14M instructions at 7,000 rows, a few milliseconds, at every pause. A kept frame now records what can show (its walk cut to its clips, searching long lists by them as a damaged walk does by its damage), and what comes into view later reaches it in a splice of what changed; a repaint of a node it left out that still can't show patches nothing (`Scene::ink_may_show`). The pause frame at 7,000 rows costs 1.9M instructions rather than 15.2M (0.28 ms rather than 1.21 ms on an M5), and a kept frame holds the same items at 1,000 rows as at 7,000. A whole repaint searches long lists by its clip too.
- **`keyed` looks each key up once a pass** (zgx): each child's entry notes the pass that last showed its key and where, so one lookup finds a key's child, refuses a key met twice and marks what stays; no set of the pass's keys is built. A row of a keyed `ui.each` built again (a selection, an edited item) costs 1.2% fewer instructions in `script-update`'s `rows` and `rows-in-place` scenarios.
- **A large subtree taken out repaints what it showed, not the whole window** (zgx): removing a subtree of more than a few hundred nodes at once (`Ui::remove_large`: a page, a long transcript) damages the area it painted, where its parent placed it and within the clips around it, instead of the whole viewport. Switching a page beside a sidebar no longer repaints the sidebar.
- **A data module of megabytes is compiled while the window is made** (zgx, zgx-script): `compile_ahead` passed over any module larger than 1 MB, so a feed kept in a Luau string compiled on the main thread at its `require`. It now takes files up to its 16 MB budget. What it compiled comes back to the worker as the load lets go of it, and the worker frees it and rests its thread as the decoding thread does (`set_decoder_rest`). mimalloc keeps pages that another thread freed for the thread that made them, and a window's peak footprint was otherwise up to 15 MB higher. A 6.8 MB feed's `require` takes 2.3-2.5 ms, not 18-20, the heavy list's first frame comes 7-19 ms sooner, and its peak footprint is 7 MB lower (M5 Max).
- **A scroll at 60 Hz shows newer motion without costing the window server more** (zgx-desktop, macOS): a scroll's frame still starts as late as it makes its refresh, but hands over at the grid's point, about 1 ms before the refresh, as other steady frames do, instead of as soon as drawn, 6.5 ms before. On an M1 Max at 60 Hz the window server's time per frame is back where it was before late frames (and 4-11% under handing over at once), with motion 2-10 ms newer than that again.
- **A paragraph with no link or icon watches nothing of where it is** (zgx): every rich-text paragraph watched its own box and its owner's, published at each layout that moved them, to place links' and icons' boxes it didn't have; it watches them once it has one. A 40 KB markdown document shown costs 10% fewer instructions.
- **Laying out a box whose size is known doesn't measure it as one whose size isn't** (zgx): an absolute box with both insets, and any child flex had already sized, was measured a second time with no width before being placed at it, with everything it holds.
- **Lists build the rows about to show off the scroll frame, and keep them** (zgx, zgx-desktop, zgx-script): a window spends the first half of each refresh, before the frame's own work, on the rows within a viewport of each edge of a list's view, the way it's going first, so a scroll frame finds them built, measured and laid out; rows that leave stay for 4 viewports more (256 rows and 8,000 nodes at most), so scrolling back builds nothing. A list that never scrolls builds what it always did, and 30 seconds after a window's last frame a list goes back to that, its memory freed, covered or not. `ahead` says how many viewports (`0`: as before). A list's rows in view are still built at once; its `overscan` is the least built ahead. `zgx inspect frames` shows this time as `spare` entries, and leaves it out of a replay's numbers.
- **`ui.list { data = s, key = "id" }` keys a long list without making its items tables** (zgx-script): the field is read in Rust, so only the rows being built reach the script. With a function, every new item is converted to read its key: at 2,000 turns of a chat, 84–88 ms a list on an M5.
- **A measured list's pass reads its rows' sizes untracked** (zgx): one revision, bumped as any content size is published resized, stands for them all. Each pass had tracked every mounted row's size, about 70 a step, each scanning the pass's dependencies.
- **A `ui.list` doesn't build rows folded to nothing** (zgx, zgx-script): rows given almost no height all fit in the viewport, so a list with thousands folded away built every one of them. With `heights`, a row under a hundredth of a point isn't built, and a row of 0 takes no room, where it was laid out a point tall.
- **A list scrolled wholly out of view works out nothing of its clips** (zgx): a list that scrolls with the view around it (`virtualize_in_ancestor`) found what of it shows from its clips every time the view moved, even when it was wholly above or below the view, as most of a long transcript's lists are. Such a list is now told past without them. On a fast scroll through 60 groups of rows (the `scroll-glass` cell) that's about 1% fewer instructions a frame.
- **A window that wakes alone at rest gives its GPU queue back at once** (zgx-desktop): a frame drawn now and then by an idle window (a relative time ticking, an update from elsewhere), with no input since its queue was last given back, gives the queue back at its trim (1.5 s after its last frame) instead of keeping it, and the ~200 MB Metal holds for it, for 10 s. A wake soon after another, or one where input acted, keeps it as before. `ZGX_QUEUE_REST_LONE=0` keeps the old behaviour.
- **A long string in state is read as often as scripts like, at the cost of one copy** (zgx-script): `get` and `peek` of a text past 64 KiB (a document, a draft) hand every read the script string the first one made, until the text changes; each read made it again, copying and hashing all of it. `state:len()` gives a string's length in bytes and `state:sub(i, j)` its bytes as `string.sub` does, read where the text is held, so a binding that needs a draft's line at the caret, or whether it's empty, never copies the rest. Zeron's composer: a keystroke in a draft of 30,000 lines from 343M instructions to 11M, the same as in a draft of 20.
- **A script's children are moved once as they lower** (zgx-script): each child's view goes from `lower` straight into a vector the parent keeps (`View::children_vec`), where it was moved again by `children`. The mount benchmark does 0.17% fewer instructions.
- **A Luau list built in the browser keeps its garbage down** (zgx-script): there, the Rust bytes each node holds step Luau's collector, which saw only a pointer-sized userdata for each. The nodes a build lowers no longer pile up to a peak that wasm memory keeps for good. A Luau table of 10,000 rows peaks at 28 MB of wasm memory rather than 38, a second build holds 21 MB live rather than 31, and creating the rows takes 3% less time. The desktop is unchanged: it gives its peaks back, and its idle trim collects them.
- **Luau no longer reads the clock per call in the browser** (zgx-script): with no watchdog thread there, every safepoint checked a call's time budget, reading `performance.now`. Now one check in 1,024 looks, and a call's budget starts at that first look, so a short call reads no clock at all. Once a call is out of time, every check looks again, so a runaway script is still stopped just past its budget. A Luau table's create of 10,000 rows takes 2.5% less script time. The desktop is unchanged.
- **A link definition no longer makes every later chunk of a streamed Markdown reply parse the whole reply** (zgx-script): what only looks like one (`[x]: y` in a code block) costs nothing, a real one with no reference ahead of it costs nothing beyond its tail, its later references following it, and one with a reference ahead of it parses the whole text as it lands. Per chunk at 800 sections, 27M instructions in update become 120K. A whole parse keeps the values of the blocks that come out the same.
- **A markdown document's blocks and runs name their fields without allocating** (zgx-script): each block and run made each of its field names anew, an allocation a name; they are made once a thread now. A 40 KB document shown costs 2.8% fewer instructions.
- **A markdown document's runs are read in one pass and its paragraphs start from one empty text** (zgx, zgx-script): each run's text, flags and link were six searches of its fields, and each paragraph mounted made an empty text of its own before its first runs. A 40 KB document shown costs 3.1% fewer instructions.
- **A streamed Markdown reply converts only its new blocks** (zgx-script): each chunk of a keyed `ui.markdown` stream answered a new list, which a script reading it converted whole, weighing and walking every block it held; its table is now derived from the last answer's, converting and weighing only what changed. A 400-section reply streamed in costs 21.5% fewer instructions (`md-stream`, a new gate cell).
- **`ui.markdown` given a state answers a state of its blocks** (zgx-script): `local blocks = ui.markdown(reply, { key = "reply" })`, drawn by `ui.each(blocks, …)`, keeps the blocks current in Rust as `reply` grows. No script string is made of the text, no script table of each answer, and a keyed stream parses a long reply (`reply:append(token)`) from what was appended alone: each chunk of a streaming reply costs what it adds. A string works as before (a table of blocks). `zgx lint` says so when a computed wraps the state form (`computed-markdown-state`), and no longer takes `ui.markdown(state:get())` in a computed for a value read once.
- **Layout keeps its measured sizes for less** (zgx): a result moved out of the few kept whole had its size checked against every size kept, one by one, to keep each constraint once, though it is nearly never there already; it is looked for first now, and taken out only when found. A node that hasn't changed looks for its new result's size the same way rather than going through them all. A dragged split's layout costs 2.8% fewer instructions; every result is the same.
- **A window's first frame on macOS no longer waits for its shaders to compile** (zgx-gpu): the shaders every first frame needs are compiled when zgx is built, where Xcode's Metal toolchain is, and loaded at start. A launch whose shader cache missed (the first after an install or a system update) drew its first frame about 150 ms later for compiling them; launches with a warm cache are as before. Built without the toolchain (Linux, a cross build, no Metal toolchain installed), or with `ZGX_NO_METALLIB` set, the shaders compile at start as before. The binary grows by about 50 KB.
- **The first code block a window shows parses one font, not every fixed-width one** (zgx-cosmic-text): asked whether the monospace family's face covered a word, a build without `monospace_fallback` found no face covering anything, so the first monospaced word parsed every fixed-width face on the system (13 on a Mac) before settling on that face anyway. Its character map answers now: a window's first frame with code in it is about 0.3–0.6 ms sooner, with the same glyphs.
- **A list animating its rows hashes their keys faster** (zgx-script): `ui.each` with motion keeps its rows in maps hashed with zgx's seeded key hasher instead of std's SipHash, and sizes them for the list up front. The script-layout benchmark does 2.4% fewer instructions.
- **A node moved with nothing under it is damaged in one step** (zgx): `Scene::set_transform` works a childless node's old and new painted areas out together from one look at it, and adds them as one when they overlap along an axis (or marks them on the damage grid), where it walked the node's subtree twice. The damage is the same rectangles. Rows animating their order (the script-layout benchmark) do 13% fewer instructions, the anime benchmark 5%.
- **A window no longer wakes for pointer moves outside it on X11** (zgx-desktop on Linux and Windows): zgx reads none of winit's device events, and now asks for none. X11 sent each move twice, once raw (XInput2) to the whole app wherever the pointer was, and Windows posted a `WM_INPUT` for each move and key of a focused window. On X11, a move outside the window cost the app about 16,000 instructions and now costs nothing, and a move over it costs about 3,600 (13%) fewer. From crates.io's winit, X11 stops raw moves once a window has had focus and lost it, not from the start.
- **A scroll works out observed bounds from the scroll view up, not from each node** (zgx): every node whose bounds are observed (`NodeHandle::observe_bounds`) keeps its translations summed up to its nearest scroll content, and a frame that only scrolls adds the scroll content's and its ancestors' to that, in the same order, so observers hear the same bounds to the bit. Any other translation of a node with children, or a node made scroll content or not, works them out again; what was kept goes at rest (`Ui::rest`). In a transcript of rows nested 30 boxes deep with their bounds observed, the bounds pass a frame went from 2.0–2.6 ms to 0.3–0.4 ms on an M5.
